--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully before designing FSMs.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering one type of item (good type), increases by 1 for the other (bad type)
- Robots need to: go to the correct half of the source (one color side), pick up item, return to nest
- The good half is either BLUE or GREEN - we don't know which, so we need to try both
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=in between), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source halves)
- Robots should go toward the correct colored wall at the source, then return to the nest (toward RED wall)

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Explore/go toward source (GoToColor toward BLUE or GREEN wall)
- State 1: Return to nest (GoToColor toward RED wall)
- Transitions: BLACK floor → picked up item → return to nest; WHITE floor → delivered → go back to source

I'll try BLUE-collecting variants and GREEN-collecting variants, plus exploration-based approaches.

**FSM designs:**
1. GoToBlue → on BLACK floor go to RED (nest); on WHITE floor go back to Blue
2. GoToGreen → on BLACK floor go to RED (nest); on WHITE floor go back to Green
3. Multi-state with exploration phase
4. Various probability and parameter tweaks

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my designs based on what should work for this mission.

**Key insight I may have missed:** The color IDs are: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So:
- RED wall (nest side) = color 1
- GREEN wall (good/bad source half) = color 2  
- BLUE wall (good/bad source half) = color 3

**Refined strategy:** Robots should go to one source half, pick up item (floor turns BLACK), then navigate back to nest (go toward RED wall). The challenge is we don't know if GREEN or BLUE gives the good item - try both. Also consider: maybe robots should AVOID the bad source half.

**Key improvement:** Use `--c<I>x<K> 7` (perceive color) to detect which wall is visible, enabling smarter decisions. Also try GoAwayColor from bad side while going to good side.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit score feedback, but I can reason about what's likely working and what isn't.

**Key reasoning:**
- The simplest 2-state machines (GoToColor source → GoToColor nest) should be the backbone
- We don't know if GREEN (color 2) or BLUE (color 3) is the "good" item - need both variants
- The transition logic matters: on BLACK floor (at source) → go to nest; on WHITE floor (at nest) → go to source
- GoAwayColor from the bad side while heading to good side might help avoid contamination
- The color 7 condition (perceive color) could help robots distinguish which side they're heading to

**Critical realization:** When a robot enters the source area, it gets an item regardless. If it enters the wrong half, it gets a bad item. So robots should specifically target ONE color side only. GoToColor toward BLUE (3) or GREEN (2) is the core behavior.

**Also:** Maybe robots should go away from the RED wall to find the source, then go toward RED to return - simpler than relying on floor color for transitions alone.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm not receiving explicit score feedback, I need to reason more carefully about what should work best.

**Critical analysis:**

The fundamental issue is I don't know which color (GREEN=2 or BLUE=3) gives the good item. The evolutionary algorithm should be finding this out. Let me think about what the best possible FSM looks like:

1. **Ideal behavior**: Go to good source half → pick up item (floor becomes BLACK) → go to nest (WHITE floor) → repeat
2. **The GoToColor behavior**: moves toward the nearest visible object of that color. At the source end, the wall blocks are either BLUE or GREEN. The robot should target one specific color wall.
3. **Transition clarity**: BLACK floor = at source, picked up item → switch to returning. WHITE floor = at nest, delivered → switch to foraging.

**New insight**: Maybe I should try GoAwayColor from RED (away from nest = toward source) combined with GoToColor RED (toward nest). This avoids needing to know which source color is good - robots just avoid the nest wall to reach source, then return. But they'd pick up random items from either half...

**Better insight**: Try using the LED color as a signal - robots carrying good items could signal to others. But that's complex.

**Simplest effective approach**: Stick with GoToColor GREEN/BLUE → GoToColor RED, but also try GoAwayColor RED → GoToColor RED as exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm still not receiving explicit score data, I need to think more carefully about what could be going wrong with my designs.

**Deep re-analysis:**

1. The score is MINIMISED. Delivering a "good" item decreases score by 1 (good), delivering a "bad" item increases score by 1 (bad). We want negative scores.

2. **Color IDs**: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. Wall colors: RED wall at nest side = color 1, BLUE wall blocks = color 3, GREEN wall blocks = color 2.

3. **Critical question**: Maybe GoToColor doesn't work well because the wall blocks are far away and robots can't always see them. The floor color transitions (BLACK/GRAY/WHITE) might be more reliable.

4. **Alternative approach**: Use floor color exclusively. Robots in GRAY → explore toward source. On BLACK → turn around, go toward WHITE. On WHITE → turn around, go toward BLACK. But without directional control this is hard...

5. **GoAwayColor from RED** = moves away from nest wall = toward source. This is reliable since RED wall is always visible from most of the arena. Then **GoToColor RED** = returns to nest.

6. Maybe the issue is that GoToColor GREEN (color 2) is making robots go to the GREEN source half which might be the BAD half! Let me emphasize trying both and also the GoAwayRED approach more strongly.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Deep rethink - I've been getting no feedback on scores, which suggests my designs may all be performing similarly (possibly all bad). Let me fundamentally reconsider.**

**Core problem reanalysis:**
- I've been trying both GREEN (2) and BLUE (3) source colors symmetrically
- Without score feedback, I don't know which is good
- But the evolutionary optimizer should be finding this - unless ALL my machines are failing

**What might be going wrong:**
1. Maybe `GoToColor` toward a distant wall color doesn't work reliably - robots may not see the wall blocks from far away
2. Maybe the floor-color transitions are firing too eagerly (p=0.95 is very high, robots might flip state on every step)
3. Maybe robots are getting stuck oscillating near the source/nest boundary
4. **Critical**: Maybe I should use GRAY floor condition to stay in state (not trigger), and only BLACK/WHITE to switch

**New strategy - use floor colors more carefully:**
- Transition probability should be high (0.9+) for BLACK→return and WHITE→forage
- But ALSO add a GRAY condition to stay exploring if not at source yet
- Try lower probabilities on color conditions to avoid noise

**Also try: pure exploration with random walk finding source, using only floor color to decide direction**

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Fundamental rethink - 7 rounds with no score feedback means I need to consider I might be making systematic errors.**

**Key realization I've been missing:**
- The problem says "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, REPLACING any item it was already carrying"
- The score decreases by 1 for ONE type and increases by 1 for the OTHER type
- We want robots to deliver the GOOD type and AVOID delivering the BAD type
- But critically: robots should also AVOID the bad source half entirely

**What if both halves are equally reachable and robots are going to both?** Then score ≈ 0 (neutral). We need to commit to ONE side only.

**Another critical issue**: Maybe my transitions are wrong. With condition C=0 (BLACK floor, probability p), the robot transitions with probability p PER STEP while on black floor. At p=0.99, it transitions almost immediately. But maybe I need p=1.0? No, max is 1.0 but I've been using 0.99.

**Wait - maybe the issue is simpler**: GoToColor toward GREEN (color 2) or BLUE (color 3) - do these colors actually appear as robot LEDs or wall colors? The camera detects colored objects including OTHER ROBOTS' LEDs. If other robots are lit with those colors, GoToColor might track robots not walls!

Let me try cleaner designs with color 7 (perceive color) conditions to detect which half robots are near, and use LED colors that don't conflict.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.99 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.99

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Complete rethink after 8 rounds with no score feedback.**

**What I think is happening:** The optimizer is running but I'm not seeing which machines score best. I need to be more systematic and cover fundamentally different design spaces.

**Core logic audit:**
- `GoToColor` with `--clr` 2=green, 3=blue: these target wall-block colors at source
- `GoAwayColor` with `--clr` 1=red: moves away from nest wall = toward source
- Floor BLACK = at source (picked up item). Floor WHITE = at nest (delivered item).
- The key behavioral loop: [go to source] → [BLACK floor detected] → [go to nest] → [WHITE floor detected] → repeat

**What if the issue is that robots need to be on GRAY to continue moving toward source?** The transition fires probabilistically each step on that floor color. With p=0.99 on BLACK, robots switch almost instantly - good. But maybe I need transitions that keep robots in the "return" state while on GRAY too.

**New idea:** Add explicit GRAY floor handling - while returning (heading to nest), if floor is GRAY, stay returning. Only switch back to foraging on WHITE.

**Also:** Try completely different topologies - e.g., Exploration-based with floor sensing only, no color-seeking.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Final round - must make best use of remaining budget.**

**After 9 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to reason about what's most likely to work and cover the key uncertainty: which color (GREEN=2 or BLUE=3) is the "good" source half.**

**What I'm most confident about:**
- The core loop is correct: go to source → detect BLACK floor → return to nest → detect WHITE floor → repeat
- `GoAwayColor RED (1)` reliably moves robots away from nest toward source
- `GoToColor RED (1)` reliably moves robots toward nest
- The color of the good source half is unknown - must try both GREEN(2) and BLUE(3)
- Using LED color 0 (off) avoids robots tracking each other's LEDs with GoToColor

**Key variants to try in final round:**
1. Pure GoAwayRed→GoToRed (no source color targeting - goes to random half)
2. GoToGreen→GoToRed (targets green half specifically)  
3. GoToBlue→GoToRed (targets blue half specifically)
4. Multi-state with exploration buffer
5. Variants with different probability thresholds

**The simplest possible foraging FSM with p=0.99 and LED off should be most reliable. Cover both color variants and GoAwayRed.**

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
